On Resolving Non-determinism in Choreographies Thanks: Research partly supported by the European Unions Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska-Curie grant agreement No 778233, by the UBACyT projects 20020170100544BA and 20020170100086BA, and by the PIP project 11220130100148CO​
Laura Bocchi \rsuper a, Hernán Melgratti \rsuper b, Emilio Tuosto \rsuper c

Abstract
. Choreographies specify multiparty interactions via message passing. A realisation of a choreography is a composition of independent processes that behave as specified by the choreography. Existing relations of correctness/completeness between choreographies and realisations are based on models where choices are non-deterministic. Resolving non-deterministic choices into deterministic choices (e.g., conditional statements) is necessary to correctly characterise the relationship between choreographies and their implementations with concrete programming languages. We introduce a notion of realisability for choreographies –called whole-spectrum implementation – where choices are still non-deterministic in choreographies, but are deterministic in their implementations. Our notion of whole spectrum implementation rules out deterministic implementations of roles that, no matter which context they are placed in, will never follow one of the branches of a non-deterministic choice. We give a type discipline for checking whole-spectrum implementations. As a case study, we analyse the POP protocol under the lens of whole-spectrum implementation.

Introduction

The context
A
choreography
describes the
expected interactions of a system in terms of the messages exchanged
among its components (aka
roles
):

“Using the Web Services Choreography specification, a contract
containing a global definition of the common ordering conditions and
constraints under which messages are exchanged, is produced
[…]. Each party can then use the global definition to build and test solutions that conform to it. The global specification is in
turn realised by combination of the resulting local systems
[…]”

The first part of the excerpt above taken from
[
30
]

envisages a choreography as a global contract regulating the exchange
of messages; the last part identifies a distinctive element of
choreographies: the global definition can be used to verify local
components (correctly) realise the global contract.
A choreography allows for the combination of independently developed
distributed components (
e.g.
, services) while hiding implementation
details.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{c}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{\scriptstyle\textsf{}}}{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{b}}\colon{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{login}}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{c}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{\scriptstyle\textsf{}}}{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{b}}\colon{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{deposit}}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{c}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{\scriptstyle\textsf{}}}{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{b}}\colon{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{overdraft}}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{b}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{\scriptstyle\textsf{}}}{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{c}}\colon{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{ko}}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{b}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{\scriptstyle\textsf{}}}{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{c}}\colon{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{ok}}}
+
+
+
+

Figure 1.
ATM
Choreography

Moreover, the communication pattern specified in the choreography
yields sufficient information to be projected so to check each
component implementing one of the roles.
For illustration, take a simple choreography, hereafter called

ATM
and expressed as the global graph
[
35
,
25
]

on
Fig.

1
, involving a customer
{\mathsf{{c}}}
and the cash
machine of a bank
{\mathsf{{b}}}
.
The nodes labelled by

+

represent branching or merging points
of choices.
After successful authentication,
{\mathsf{{b}}}
offers a deposit and an
overdraft service to
{\mathsf{{c}}}
.
In the global graph this choice corresponds to the topmost

+

branching over the next interactions between the
participants.
The bank
{\mathsf{{b}}}
can either grant or deny overdrafts asked by the
customer
{\mathsf{{c}}}
.

On realisations

A set of processes is a
realisation
of a choreography when the
behaviour emerging from their distributed execution matches the
behaviour specified by the choreography.
A choreography is
realisable
when it has a realisation.

A realisation of
ATM
can, for example, be given using two CCS-like
processes
[
38
]
(augmented with internal
\_\oplus\_
and
external
\_+\_
choice operators) for roles
{\mathsf{{b}}}
and
{\mathsf{{c}}}
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ = \ }l}\hskip 42.67912pt\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}.(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}\ +\ \mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}.(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}}}\ \oplus\ \overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}}))\\[10.00002pt]
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{c}}}}&\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}}.(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}}\ \oplus\ \overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}}.(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}}\ +\ \mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}))\end{array}

In words,
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
specifies that, after
{\mathsf{{c}}}
logs in,
{\mathsf{{b}}}
waits to interact either on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}
or on

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}
; in the latter case,
{\mathsf{{b}}}
non-deterministically
decides whether to grant or deny the overdraft;
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{c}}}}
is the
dual of
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
.
Note that
ATM
uses non-determinism to avoid specifying the criteria
for
{\mathsf{{b}}}
to grant or deny an overdraft.
The use of non-determinism is also reflected in realisations, in fact

\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
uses the internal choice operator
\_\oplus\_
to
model the reaction when
{\mathsf{{c}}}
requests an overdraft.

Choreographies can be interpreted either as
constraints
or as

obligations
of distributed
interactions
[
37
]
.
The former interpretation
(aka
partial

[
37
]
or
weak
[
43
]
) admits a realisation if it
exhibits a subset of the behaviour.
We propose WSI as a criterion to assess if an implementation takes
into account all the execution cases prescribed by a choreography.
The theory can be used in practice, to implement a static verification
checker targeted at specific languages, and possibly embedded in a
(behavioural) type checker (e.g., for Java
[
27
]
or
Haskel
[
39
]
), or an API generation tool (e.g.,
for Java
[
28
]
or F#
[
40
]
). The aforementioned
tools are generally targeted at checking soundness of implementations,
and checking for WSI would add guarantees of ‘completeness’. In this
paper, we use a process algebra as an abstraction of implementations,
instead of a full fledged programming language, to simplify the
presentation and the development of the theory.
For instance, take

\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}.(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}\ +\ \mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}})

then
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{c}}}}
form a partial realisation of

ATM
where overdraft requests are consistently denied.
On the contrary, when interpreting choreographies as obligations, a
realisation is admissible if it is able to exhibit
all

interaction sequences (hence such realisations are also referred to as

complete
realisations
[
37
]
).

For instance,
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{c}}}}
form a complete
realisation of
ATM
.

The problem

Typically, realisations are non-deterministic specifications; here we
explore the problem of resolving their non-determinism.
In fact, despite being a valuable abstraction mechanism,
non-determinism has to be implemented using deterministic constructs
such as conditional statements.

Using again
ATM
, we illustrate that traditional notions of complete
realisation are not fully satisfactory.
The non-deterministic choice in
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
abstracts away from the
actual conditions used in implementations to resolve the choice.
This permits a bank to adopt different policies depending
e.g.
, on the
type of the clients’ accounts.
Consider the (deterministic) implementations
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
of

\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
below written as value-passing CCS processes:

\begin{array}[]{lcll}B_{i}&::=&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}(c).\left(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}(x).Q+\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}(x).P_{i}(c)\right)&\text{for }i=1,2\quad(Q\text{ is immaterial})\\[1.20007pt]
P_{1}(c)&::=&~{\mathit{check}(c)}~~\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}}}~~{\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}}}\\[1.20007pt]
P_{2}(c)&::=&\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}}\end{array}

Both
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
expect to receive the login credentials
c
of a
client on channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}
.
After that, they offer the services
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}
and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}
.
The implementations differ in the way they handle overdraft requests,
which are respectively defined by
P_{1}(c)
and
P_{2}(c)
.
The expression
\mathit{check}(c)
in
P_{1}(c)
deterministically
discriminates if the overdraft should be granted depending on the
login credentials
c
provided by the client. Differently,
P_{2}(c)

refuses any overdraft request.
It is not hard to see that both
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are suitable
implementations of
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
in partial realisations of the
choreography
1
1

1

For instance, both
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
type-check
against
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
considered as a session type due to the fact
that subtyping for session types
[
23
]
is contra-variant with
respect to internal choices (and covariant with respect to external
choices).
(as

e.g.
in
[
20
]
).

Conversely, neither
B_{1}
nor
B_{2}
can be used in a complete
realisation.
This is straightforward for
B_{2}
(unable to interact over

\mathsf{ok}
after receiving an overdraft request), but not so
evident for
B_{1}
.
Depending on the credentials
c
sent by the customer at login time,

\mathit{check}(c)
will evaluate either to
\mathtt{true}
or to
\mathtt{false}
.
Therefore,
B_{1}
will execute only one of the branches.
This will be the case for any possible deterministic implementation of

ATM
: only one branch will be matched. Consequently, there is not a
complete, deterministic realisation for
ATM
.

We prefer
B_{1}
to
B_{2}
arguing that they are not equally appealing
when interpreting choreographies as obligations.
In fact,
B_{2}
consistently precludes one of the alternatives while

B_{1}
guarantees only one or the other alternative (provided that

\mathit{check}
is not a constant function) depending on the
deterministic implementation of the role
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{c}}}}
.

Contributions and synopsis

We introduce
whole-spectrum implementation
(WSI), a new
interpretation of choreographies as interaction obligations.
A WSI of a role
{\mathsf{{r}}}
guarantees that, whenever the choreography
allows
{\mathsf{{r}}}
to make an internal choice, there is a context (i.e.,
an implementation of the remaining roles) for which (the
implementation of)
{\mathsf{{r}}}
chooses such alternative.
Through the paper, we illustrate the use of WSI to analyse the POP2
protocol (i.e., choreography
Section

3
, implementation

Section

5
, and verification
Section

7.3
).

In the following, we use an elaboration of global types
from
[
34
]
to model choreographies. Implementations of
choreographies are abstracted as systems, which are parallel
compositions of processes in an asynchronous calculus.

Whole-spectrum implementation
(WSI) is defined in terms of the
denotational semantics of global types and systems.
A key point on the characterisation of WSI is the distinction between
mandatory and optional behaviour arising from the implementation of
loops.
The denotational semantics of a global type
\mathcal{G}
is given by the set

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G})
of traces describing mandatory and optional behaviour
(
6.6
in
Section

6
).
The denotation of a system
S
is also a set
\mathcal{R}(S)
of traces
(
6.4
in
Section

6
), which however do not
discriminate optional behaviour.
WSI is defined as a
covering
relation
\Subset
between the
traces of implementations
\mathcal{R}(S)
and those of global types

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G})
(
6.8
,
Section

6
).

We devise a behavioural typing framework for checking WSI.
Our global and local types are in
Section

2
; the language for
implementations is in
Section

4
.
Our typing discipline is introduced in
Section

7
.
As usual, a type judgement
\vdash S\triangleright\Delta
says that an
implementation
S
has local types
\Delta
, obtained by projecting a
global type
\mathcal{G}
(cf.
Section

2
).
We show a theorem of subject reduction and one of conformance
(
2
and
4
in
Section

8.1
) which
guarantee that well-typed systems do not deviate from the behaviour
specified by their type.
Both results rely on the operational semantics of systems
(
Section

4
) and of local types (
Section

8.1
).
We show the adequacy of the denotational semantics of systems to their
operational semantics in
6.5
(
Section

6
).
Analogously, we establish the correspondence between the operational
and denotational semantics of local types
(
6
and
5
,

Section

8.2
).

The proof that our type system ensures WSI is given in two steps: we
prove that

•

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G})\Subset\mathcal{R}(\Delta)

(
7
in

Section

8.2
), namely that the traces of the local types
projected from a global type
\mathcal{G}
cover the traces of
\mathcal{G}
; and that

•

\mathcal{R}(\Delta)\Subset\mathcal{R}(S)

(
8
in
Section

8.2
), namely
that the traces of well-typed systems cover the traces of their
local types.

By transitivity of
\Subset
, we obtain
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G})\Subset\mathcal{R}(S)
, which entails WSI for well-typed implementations
(
9
in
Section

8.2
).

The main contributions in this paper can be diagrammatically presented
as follows:

\vdash
S^{\prime}
\triangleright
\Delta^{\prime}
\vdash
S
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G})
\mathcal{R}(S)
\triangleright
\Delta
\mathcal{R}(\Delta)
\mathcal{G}

projection

2

6.4

6.6
,

\Subset

This is an extended version of
[
4
]
.
Besides giving detailed proofs, we simplified some definitions and
typing rules.
In particular,
Section

7.1
was not included in
[
4
]
and
was added here to remove communication effects from judgements.
We also improved the general presentation and refined the running
example.

2.
Global and Local Types

Our types elaborate from
[
34
]
and use a form of iteration,
which is more tractable than recursion.
We fix three countably infinite and pairwise disjoint sets:

•

\mathbb{U}
of
shared
names (ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}
),

•

\mathbb{Y}
of
session
channels (ranged over by
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}s}},\ldots
), and

•

\mathbb{P}
of
(participants) roles
ranged over by
{\mathsf{{p}}},{\mathsf{{q}}},{\mathsf{{r}}},\ldots
; it is convenient to assume that
\mathbb{P}

is (isomorphic to) the set of natural numbers.

Basic data types, called
sorts
, (
e.g.
, booleans
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Bool}}
,
integers
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}
, strings
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}
, singleton
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Unit}}
, record
types, etc.) are assumed;
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}
ranges over sorts.
We use
sorted channels

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}
to specify that channel

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\in\mbox{$\mathbb{Y}$}
is used to exchange data of sort
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}
.
We write
\vec{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
for a finite sequence of
elements
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in Z
for a given set
Z
; when
no confusion arises,
\vec{z}
may also denote the set

\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\}
(
e.g.
, we write
z_{2}\in\vec{z}
).

A
global type term
(GTT, for short)
\mathsf{G}
is derived by the
following grammar:

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}

\displaystyle::=

\displaystyle\sum_{\in}{\mathsf{{}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{}}}\colon\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{}_{\mathit{}}}};\qquad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\qquad\mathsf{G};\mathsf{G}\qquad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\qquad\mathsf{G}^{*^{f}}\qquad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\qquad\mathsf{end}

A GTT
\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i}

denotes the branching of interactions from a unique selector
{\mathsf{{p}}}

to participants
{\mathsf{{q}}}_{i}
for
i\in I\neq\emptyset
; we tacitly
assume that
{\mathsf{{p}}}\neq{\mathsf{{q}}}_{i}
for all
i\in I
, and that
{\mathsf{{q}}}_{i}={\mathsf{{q}}}_{j}
implies
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{j}
for all
i\neq j\in I

(namely that channels of a same receiver in two different branches are
different).
For a singleton
I=\{n\}
,

{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{n}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{n}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{n}}}};\mathsf{G}_{n}
shortens

\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i}
.
A communication over a channel of sort
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Unit}}
is a pure
synchronisation and usually we write
{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};\mathsf{G}
instead
of
{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Unit}}}};\mathsf{G}
.
A GTT can also be the sequential (
\_;\_
) composition of two GTTs,
the iteration of a GTT (
\_^{*^{\_}}
), or the empty term
\mathsf{end}
.
We omit trailing occurrences of
\mathsf{end}
and write
{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}}

instead of
{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};\mathsf{end}
.

As in
[
10
]
, we adopt iteration in
global types.
Function
f
in
\mathsf{G}^{*^{f}}
is an injective map that assigns
sorted channels to roles.
The mapping is used to indicate how the termination of the iteration
is communicated to the roles. More precisely, if
f({\mathsf{{p}}})=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}
then the participant
{\mathsf{{p}}}
will receive a message of
type
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}
on channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
when the iteration terminates.
We use
\mathtt{ch}(f)
for the set of channels in the image of
f
,
namely

\displaystyle\mathtt{ch}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\;\big|\;f({\mathsf{{p}}})=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{{p}}}\in\mathrm{dom}{(f)}\}

Example 2.1

(Iterative GTT)
.

We revisit the scenario introduced in
Section

1
, which
involves a client
{\mathsf{{c}}}
and a bank
{\mathsf{{b}}}
. The GTT
\mathsf{G}
below
defines a protocol in which
{\mathsf{{c}}}
, after being logged in, may
perform zero or more deposits and overdrafts.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }ll}\mathsf{G}={\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{b}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}};(\\
&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{b}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}}\\
&+\\
&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{b}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}};({\mathsf{{b}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}}+{\mathsf{{b}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}})\quad)^{*^{{\mathsf{{b}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}}}\end{array}

The protocol starts with the interaction

{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{b}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}}
, i.e.,
{\mathsf{{c}}}
sends to

{\mathsf{{b}}}
its login information (of sort
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}
) over channel

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}
.
Then, the protocol continues as an iterative GTT in which
{\mathsf{{c}}}

decides to either perform a deposit, ask for an overdraft, or
finalise the protocol. The protocol can be finalised by
{\mathsf{{c}}}

by sending to
{\mathsf{{b}}}
a message (of omitted sort
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Unit}}
) on
channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}
, as indicated by the function

{\mathsf{{b}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}
used to decorate the iterative type.
In the interaction
{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{b}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}}
,

{\mathsf{{c}}}
requests a deposit by sending the amount to be deposited
over channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}
.
In the interaction

{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{b}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}}
,
{\mathsf{{c}}}
requests an overdraft.
Note that in the case of overdraft request
{\mathsf{{c}}}
waits for a
notification from
{\mathsf{{b}}}
on whether the request is granted or
denied (a message over
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}}
or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}
, respectively,
of the omitted sort
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Unit}}
).
Once the chosen branch has been completed, the iteration can be
restarted.

\diamond

We introduce some useful auxiliary notions.
For a GTT
\mathsf{G}

•

the set of
participants
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})
of
\mathsf{G}

is

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{{\mathsf{{p}}}\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}(\{{\mathsf{{q}}}_{i}\}\cup\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G}_{i}))

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G};\mathsf{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})\cup\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G}^{*^{f}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{end})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\emptyset

•

the set of participants ready to send a message, or

ready participants
\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})
of
\mathsf{G}
is

\displaystyle\mathtt{rdy}(\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{{\mathsf{{p}}}\}

\displaystyle\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G};\mathsf{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})&\text{ if }\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})\neq\emptyset\\
\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G}^{\prime})&\text{ if }\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})=\emptyset\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G}^{*^{f}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})

\displaystyle\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{end})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\emptyset

•

the set of
channel names
\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G})\subseteq\mbox{$\mathbb{Y}$}
of

\mathsf{G}
is

\displaystyle\mathtt{ch}(\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\}

\displaystyle\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G};\mathsf{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G})\cup\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G}^{*^{f}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G})\cup\mathtt{ch}(f)

\displaystyle\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{end})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\emptyset

Example 2.2
.

The set

\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G})=\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}\}
is the set of channels used by
the choreography
\mathsf{G}
in
2.1
.
Also, we have the set of participants

\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})=\{{\mathsf{{b}}},{\mathsf{{c}}}\}
, where
{\mathsf{{c}}}
is the
one that can initiate the interaction, i.e.,

\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})=\{{\mathsf{{c}}}\}
.

\diamond

A
global type
is defined by an equation

{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G})\subseteq\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathbb{Y}$}\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\text{ are pairwise distinct names}

We abbreviate
{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}
with
\mathcal{G}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})
when
\mathsf{G}
is immaterial;
we write
\mathcal{G}
or
\mathsf{G}
instead of
\mathcal{G}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})
when parameters
are understood.

For technical reasons (
cf.

Section

7
), our global types are
explicitly parameterised on session channels; however, they will be
considered equivalent up-to renaming of parameters.
More precisely, let
\equiv_{\text{GTT}}
be the structural congruence
relation on GTTs such that

•

\_;\_
and
\_+\_
form monoids with identity
\mathsf{end}

•

and
\_+\_
is commutative;

we say that
{\mathcal{G}_{1}}({\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}})\,\triangleq\,{\mathsf{G}_{1}}
and

{\mathcal{G}_{2}}({\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}z}}}})\,\triangleq\,{\mathsf{G}_{2}}
are structurally equivalent
(written
\mathcal{G}_{1}\equiv\mathcal{G}_{2}
) when
\mathsf{G}_{1}\equiv_{\text{GTT}}\mathsf{G}_{2}\{\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}z}}}/\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\}
where, as usual,

\{\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}}}/\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\}
is the capture avoiding
substitution replacing the
i
-th element of
\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}
with the

i
-th element of
\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}z}}}
(for which we assume
\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}

to be a tuple of pairwise distinct names of the same length as

\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}z}}}
).

The extensions of
\mathcal{P}(\_)
and
\mathtt{rdy}(\_)
to
{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}
are
straightforward:
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})
and
\mathtt{rdy}(\mathcal{G})=\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})
.

As customary in session types, we restrict our attention to

well-formed
global types in order to rule out specifications
that cannot be implemented distributively.
We borrow from
[
26
,
10
]
the
standard wellformedness conditions of
knowledge of choice
and

linearity
.

Knowledge of choice requires a unique-selector in a choice and that
any other participant can determine the chosen branch from the
received messages.
For instance, the global type

{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};\mathsf{G}_{1}~~+~~{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{r}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{2}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};\mathsf{G}_{2}

violates the knowledge of choice condition: although there is a
unique-selector
{\mathsf{{p}}}
in the choice (hence the first part of the
condition is satisfied), there is a participant
{\mathsf{{q}}}
that cannot
determine the chosen branch.
More precisely,
{\mathsf{{q}}}
is ready to receive a message on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1}

either from
{\mathsf{{p}}}
in the first branch or from
{\mathsf{{s}}}
on the
second one; hence
{\mathsf{{q}}}
cannot determine which branch has been
selected after the input on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1}
.

Linearity requires absence of communication races on channels.
Races occur when causally unrelated interactions happen on a same
channel.
Consider

{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{r}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}}

where the two sent actions yield a race on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
since they are
concurrent because performed by different senders.
On the contrary,

{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{q}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{r}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}}

satisfies linearity because the two interactions on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
are
executed sequentially, hence causally related.

We introduce an additional wellformedness condition
(Definition
2.3
) that is specific to our form of
iteration.

Definition 2.3

(Well-formed iteration)
.

A GTT of the form
\mathsf{G}^{f}
is a well-formed iteration if:

(1)

\mathtt{ch}(f)\cap\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G})=\emptyset
,

(2)

\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})
is a singleton (we call the participant in

\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})
the
iteration-controller of
\mathsf{G}
) and

\mathrm{dom}{(f)}=\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})\backslash\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G})
,

(3)

for any proper subterm
\mathsf{G}_{1}^{*^{f_{1}}}
of
\mathsf{G}
,

\mathtt{ch}(f_{1})\cap\mathtt{ch}(f)=\emptyset
.

By condition (
1
), the channels used to terminate an
iteration are disjoint from those in the body
\mathsf{G}
.
This is fundamental to avoid confusion when implementing iterations
and resembles the condition about knowledge of choices.
Consider the global type

({\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};\mathsf{G}_{1})^{*^{{\mathsf{{q}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}};\mathsf{G}_{2}

that violates condition (
1
).
Note that after receiving a message from
{\mathsf{{p}}}
on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
,
{\mathsf{{q}}}

is unable to determine whether it should behave as specified by

\mathsf{G}_{1}
(i.e., to perform the body of the iteration) or
\mathsf{G}_{2}

(i.e., to exit the iteration).

Condition (
2
) requires a unique role (the
iteration-controller) to be the one deciding whether to execute the
iteration body or to terminate by notifying all other participants in
global type.
This condition avoids situations in which a participant is unaware of
the fact that some iteration has been finalised.
For example, in the following GTT

({\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{q}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{r}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{2}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}})^{*^{{\mathsf{{q}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{3}}};\mathsf{G}_{2}

{\mathsf{{r}}}
will not receive any message when
{\mathsf{{p}}}
decides to
conclude the iteration.

Finally, condition (
3
) prevents interference between
terminations of nested iterations. Consider

(({\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}})^{*^{{\mathsf{{q}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{2}}};{\mathsf{{q}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{r}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{3}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}})^{*^{{\mathsf{{q}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{2},{\mathsf{{r}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{4}}};\mathsf{G}_{2}

Note that after receiving a message on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{2}
,
{\mathsf{{q}}}
is unable to
determine if
{\mathsf{{p}}}
has concluded the inner or the outer iteration.

Hereafter, we will assume that for every
{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}
,
\mathsf{G}
satisfies
knowledge of choice and linearity (form
[
26
,
10
]
), and that every
iterative GTT appearing in
\mathsf{G}
is a well-formed iteration.

A
local type term
(LTT for short)
\mathsf{T}
is derived from the
following grammar:

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}\ ::=\ \displaystyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{T}_{i}}\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{T}_{i}}\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad\mathsf{T}_{1};\mathsf{T}_{2}\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad\mathsf{T}^{\star}\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad\mathsf{end}

An LTT is either an internal (
\bigoplus
) or external (
\sum
)
guarded choice on non-empty index sets
I
, the sequential composition

\_;\_
, an iteration
\_^{\star}
, or the empty term
\mathsf{end}
.
We usually omit trailing occurrences of
\mathsf{end}
and write

\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{n}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{n}};\mathsf{T}_{n}
(resp.

{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{n}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{n}};\mathsf{T}_{n}
) for

\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{T}_{i}}

(resp.
\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{T}_{i}}
) when
I=\{n\}
.

We assume that all channels appearing in the guards of an internal or
an external choice are pairwise different. Similarly to GTT, the set

\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{T})
of
channels
of an LTT
\mathsf{T}
is defined as

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{ch}(\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{T}_{i}})\quad=\quad\mathtt{ch}(\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{T}_{i}})\quad=\quad\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\;\big|\;i\in I\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{T}_{i})\\[20.00003pt]
\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{T}_{1};\mathsf{T}_{2})=\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{T}_{1})\cup\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{T}_{2})\qquad\qquad\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{T}^{\star})=\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{T})\qquad\qquad\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{end})=\emptyset\end{array}

A
local type
is defined by an equation

{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\mathtt{ch}(\mathsf{G})\subseteq\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathbb{Y}$}\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\text{ are pairwise distinct names}

We abbreviate
{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}
with
\mathcal{T}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})
when
\mathsf{T}
is immaterial
and we write
\mathcal{T}
or
\mathsf{T}
instead of
\mathcal{T}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})
when
parameters are understood.

The structural congruence on LTTs is defined as the smallest
congruence
\equiv_{\text{LTT}}
such that

•

internal and external choice operators are associative,
commutative and have
\mathsf{end}
as identity,

•

and
\_;\_
is associative and has
\mathsf{end}
as neutral element.

Two local types
{\mathcal{T}_{1}}({\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}_{1}})\,\triangleq\,{\mathsf{T}_{1}}
and

{\mathcal{T}_{2}}({\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}_{2}})\,\triangleq\,{\mathsf{T}_{2}}
are structurally equivalent
(written
\mathcal{T}_{1}\equiv\mathcal{T}_{2}
) when

\mathsf{T}_{1}\equiv_{\text{LTT}}\mathsf{T}_{2}\{\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}_{1}/\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}_{2}\}
.
In the following, we consider types up-to structural congruence.

The
projection
operation extracts local
types from a global type; we restrict such operation on well-formed
global types.
Given a participant
{\mathsf{{r}}}\in\mbox{$\mathbb{P}$}
, the
projection
of a
well-formed GTT
\mathsf{G}
on
{\mathsf{{r}}}
, denoted as
\mathsf{G}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{r}}}}
, is
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{end}&\text{if }{\mathsf{{r}}}\not\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})\\
\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{G}_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}}}&\text{if }\mathsf{G}=\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{{r}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i}}\\
\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{i}}.\mathsf{G}_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}}}+\sum_{i\in I\setminus I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}}{\mathsf{G}_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}}}&\text{if }\mathsf{G}=\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}}{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i}+\sum_{i\in I\setminus I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}}{\mathsf{{p}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{q_{i}}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{\mathit{i}}}};\mathsf{G}_{i}}\\
&{\mathsf{{r}}}\neq{\mathsf{{p}}},\text{ and }I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}=\{i\in I\;\big|\;{\mathsf{{q}}}_{i}={\mathsf{{r}}}\}\\
\mathsf{G}_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}};\mathsf{G}_{2}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}}&\text{if }\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}_{1};\mathsf{G}_{2}\\
(\mathsf{G}_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}})^{\star};\overline{b_{1}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{1}};\ldots;\overline{b_{n}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{n}}&\text{if }\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}_{1}^{*^{f}},~\bigcup\{f({\mathsf{{p}}})\,|\,{\mathsf{{p}}}\in\mathtt{ch}(f)\}=\{b_{1}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{n}\},\\
&\text{and }{\mathsf{{r}}}\in\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G}_{1})\\
(\mathsf{G}_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}})^{\star};{b}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}}&\text{if }\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}_{1}^{*^{f}},~f({\mathsf{{r}}})=b\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}\end{cases}

When projecting a global type
\mathsf{G}
over a participant name
{\mathsf{{r}}}
that does not appear
in it, produces the idle local type
\mathsf{end}
. The remaining cases implicitly assume that

{\mathsf{{r}}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G})
.

The projection of the (unique) selector of a branch results in the
internal choice on the session channels.
Dually, the projection on a receiver in a branch results in an
external choice; observe that branches where the receiver is not

{\mathsf{{r}}}
(i.e.,
i\in I\setminus I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}
) are treated
differently from those where the receiver is
{\mathsf{{r}}}
(i.e.,

i\in I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}
).
We remark that the projected local type is well-defined when
\mathsf{G}

is well-formed: the condition about knowledge of choices ensures
that
{\mathsf{{r}}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{G}_{i})
for all

i\in I\setminus I_{{\mathsf{{r}}}}
; moreover,
{\mathsf{{r}}}
is the receiver in
the first interaction of each branch
\mathsf{G}_{i}
.

The projection of a sequential composition is self-explanatory.
Iterative GTTs are projected depending on whether the role is a
controller or not. For
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}_{1}^{*^{f}}
, recall that (by
well-formedness) there is an iteration-controller

{\mathsf{{r}}}\in\mathtt{rdy}(\mathsf{G}_{1})
; the projection of
\mathsf{G}
on the
controller generates an iterative local type, which corresponds to
the projection of
\mathsf{G}_{1}
, followed by the messages that signal the
termination of the iteration to the remaining participants (i.e.,
the messages
\overline{b_{1}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{1}};\ldots;\overline{b_{n}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}_{n}}
).
Dually, the projection on the other participants waits for the
signal to exit the iteration (i.e.,
{b}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{d}}}
).
The
projection

\mathcal{G}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}}
of a global type

{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}
with respect to
{\mathsf{{r}}}
is a local type
{}({})\,\triangleq\,{}
where
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{G}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{\mathsf{{r}}}
.

Example 2.4
.

Consider the GTT
\mathsf{G}
introduced in
2.1
.
Since
\mathsf{G}
consists of a single branch where
{\mathsf{{c}}}
is the
selector, the projection on
{\mathsf{{c}}}
is an internal choice with a
single branch that sends a message on channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}
and
follows with the projection of the iterative type in the
continuation.
Since
{\mathsf{{c}}}
is the interation-controller of the continuation,
its projection consists of the iteration of the body followed by
the termination message
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}
, as shown below.

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{c}}}}=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}.(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}+\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}.({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}}}\ {}+{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}}\ {}))^{\star};\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}}\ {}

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{b}}}}=

\displaystyle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}login}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}.({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}deposit}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}+{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}overdraft}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}.(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ok}}}\ {}+\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ko}}}\ {}))^{\star};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}}\ {}

Note that
\mathsf{G}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
and
\mathsf{G}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{b}}}}
are each other’s dual.

\diamond

3.
Types for the POP2 Protocol

We illustrate our approach on the Post Office Protocol Version 2
(POP2)
[
6
]
between a client
{\mathsf{{c}}}
and a mail server
{\mathsf{{s}}}
.
We describe POP2 with the following global type:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}&\,\triangleq&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}\ +\ {\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}helo}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}};{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}\\[1.13809pt]
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}&\,\triangleq&{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}bye}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}}\end{array}

The global type
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}
starts with
{\mathsf{{c}}}
sending a message to

{\mathsf{{s}}}
either on channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}
(of the omitted sort

{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Unit}}
) or on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}helo}}
to communicate its password (of sort

{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}
).
In the first case, the interaction ends after
{\mathsf{{s}}}
sends a message
on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}bye}}
as per
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}
; in the latter case the
protocol follows as per
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}
below.

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}&\,\triangleq&{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}e}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}\ +\ {\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}};{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}}\end{array}

In
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}
,
{\mathsf{{s}}}
either signals an error on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}e}}

before terminating or sends a message on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}
containing the
number of messages in the default mailbox and then continues as
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{}l@{}r@{\ }l}{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}&\,\triangleq\,(&&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}fold}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}\\
&&+&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}read}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}};{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}})^{*{\mathsf{{s}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}};{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}}\end{array}

where
{\mathsf{{c}}}
repeatedly requests either (a) the number of messages
available in a folder, or (b) the length of a particular message in
the current folder.
The iteration-controller is
{\mathsf{{c}}}
and it uses
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}
to
communicate the termination of the loop to
{\mathsf{{s}}}
.
In case (a),
{\mathsf{{c}}}
sends the folder’s name over the channel

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}fold}}
and waits for the answer on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}
; after this, the
body of the iteration is completed and the loop can be repeated
again. In case (b),
{\mathsf{{c}}}
sends the index corresponding to the
selected message on channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}read}}
and waits for the answer on
channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}
; after this, the interaction continues as
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}}
specified below:

\begin{array}[]{l@{}l@{}r@{\ }l}{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}}&\,\triangleq\,(&&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}read}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}\\
&&+&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}retr}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}msg}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Data}}.{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{xfer}$}})^{*{\mathsf{{s}}}\mapsto\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}fold}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}\end{array}

In
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}}
, another loop controlled by
{\mathsf{{c}}}
lets the
client either (a) ask for another message by interacting again on

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}read}}
as described above or (b) retrieve a message.
In the latter case,
{\mathsf{{c}}}
signals on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}retr}}
that it is ready
to receive the message, which is then sent back on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}msg}}
by

{\mathsf{{s}}}
(sort
{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Data}}
abstracts away the format of messages
specified in
[
17
]
).
Finally,
{\mathsf{{c}}}
acknowledges the reception of the requested message
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{xfer}$}}&\,\triangleq&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}acks}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}\\
&+&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ackd}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}\\
&+&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}nack}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}\end{array}

Basically
{\mathsf{{c}}}
may use one among three alternative channels:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}acks}}
to acknowledge the reception of the message,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ackd}}
to keep the message or,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}nack}}
to notify that the
message has not been received properly (in which case the message is
kept in the mailbox).
In each case,
{\mathsf{{s}}}
sends back to
{\mathsf{{c}}}
the length of the next
message over channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}
.

The local type
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}
obtained by projecting
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}
on the
participant
{\mathsf{{s}}}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}={{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{s}}}}
, is
below.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ \eqdef\ } l}\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}\hfil\ \,\triangleq\,&{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}}\ {}.{{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}}\ +\ {\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}helo}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}\hfil\ \,\triangleq\,&\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}bye}}}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}\hfil\ \,\triangleq\,&\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}e}}}\ {}.{{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}}\ \oplus\ \overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}.{{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}\hfil\ \,\triangleq\,&({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}fold}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}\ +\ {\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}read}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}})^{*};{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}}\ {};{{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}}}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}}\hfil\ \,\triangleq\,&({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}read}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}\ +\ {\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}retr}}}\ {}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}msg}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Data}}}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{xfer}$}})^{*};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}fold}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}};\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{int}}}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{xfer}$}}\hfil\ \,\triangleq\,&{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}acks}}}\ {}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}\ +\ {\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}ackd}}}\ {}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}\ +\ {\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}nack}}}\ {}.\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}\end{array}

Note that the messages in
\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}
are as in
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}
. We remark
that
{\mathsf{{s}}}
does not control any of the two iterations (i.e.,

{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}
and
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}}
), hence the projections iterate
until
{\mathsf{{s}}}
receive a signal on the termination channels:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}
in
{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}
and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}fold}}
in

{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{size}$}}
, respectively.

The projection
{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{c}}}}
of
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}
on
{\mathsf{{c}}}
is
obtained analogously; the resulting local type is the dual of

\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}
, i.e., the one obtained by substituting internal
choices by external ones and vice versa.

The projection
{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{c}}}}
of
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}
onto
{\mathsf{{c}}}
is
obtained analogously; the resulting local type is the dual of

\mathsf{T}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}
, i.e., the one obtained by substituting internal
choices by external ones and vice versa.

For illustrative purpose, in the next example we present a multiparty
variant of
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}
, where the authentication is outsourced.

Example 3.1
.

A multiparty variant of POP2 is defined by the global type
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}^{\prime}

below.

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}^{\prime}&\,\triangleq&{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}}~+~{\mathsf{{c}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}helo}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}};{\mathsf{G}^{\prime}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}\\[1.13809pt]
{\mathsf{G}^{\prime}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}&\,\triangleq&{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{a}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}req}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}};{\mathsf{{a}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{s}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}res}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Bool}};\big({\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}e}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{}};{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}}~+~{\mathsf{{s}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\mathsf{{c}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}};{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}}\big)\end{array}

In this version,
{\mathsf{{s}}}
uses a third-party authentication service

{\mathsf{{a}}}
, which is contacted immediately after the server receives a

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}helo}}
message from the client.
The server sends to
{\mathsf{{a}}}
an authentication request over

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}res}}
and waits for the authorisation on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}res}}
(
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}
and
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}
remain unchanged).

The following equations

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{T}^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}&\,\triangleq&{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}quit}}}\ {}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}~+~{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}helo}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{AUTH}$}}\\
{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{AUTH}$}}&\,\triangleq&\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}req}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Str}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}res}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Bool}}}.{\mathsf{T}^{\prime}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}\\
{\mathsf{T}^{\prime}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{MBOX}$}}&\,\triangleq&\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}e}}}\ {}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{EXIT}$}}~\oplus~\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}r}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.543,0.4883}\mathsf{Int}}}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{NMBR}$}}\end{array}

yield
the projection
\mathsf{T}^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{{s}}}}{{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}^{\prime}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{\mathsf{{s}}}}
of
{\mathsf{G}}_{\resizebox{}{196608}{$\mathtt{POP}$}}^{\prime}
on
{\mathsf{{s}}}
.

\diamond

4.
Processes and Systems

Choreographies specify distributed applications that we refer to as

systems
. Concretely, systems are the parallel composition of

processes
realising the roles in a choreography.

Processes manipulate and exchange values obtained by evaluating
expressions.
Let
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{V}
be two infinite disjoint sets of

variables
and
basic values
respectively which are both
disjoint from the sets of shared names
\mathbb{U}
, session channels

\mathbb{Y}
, and participants
\mathbb{P}
.
Values are specified by
expressions
having the following
syntax:

e\ \ ::=\ \ x\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\mathsf{v}\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;e_{1}\mathtt{~bop~}e_{2}\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\mathtt{~uop~}e\qquad\qquad\ell\ \ ::=\ \ [e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}]\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;e_{1}..e_{2}

An expression
e
is either a variable
x\in\mbox{$\mathbb{X}$}
or a value

\mathsf{v}\in\mbox{$\mathbb{V}$}
, or else the composition
e_{1}\mathtt{~bop~}e_{2}
of two
expressions through a binary operator
\mathtt{~bop~}
​, or the application of
a unary operator
\mathtt{~uop~}
to an expression (operators are left
unspecified and can be thought of as the usual logical-arithmetic
operators of programming languages).
We assume that expressions are implicitly sorted and, for simplicity,
our expressions do not include binders of variables, names, or test
for definiteness.
Lists
[e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}]
and numerical ranges
e_{1}..e_{2}
are used
for iteration; in the latter case, both expressions
e_{1}
and
e_{2}

are of sort integer.
The empty list is denoted as
\varepsilon
and the operations

headLcolor]{hd}(\ell)
and
headLcolor]{tl}(\ell)
respectively return the head and
tail of
\ell
(defined as usual).
Given an expression
e
or a list
\ell
, the sets
\mathrm{var}({e})
and

\mathrm{var}({\ell})
of
variables of
e
and
\ell
respectively, are
defined as

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{var}({x})=\{x\}\quad\mathrm{var}({\mathsf{v}})=\emptyset\quad\mathrm{var}({\mathtt{~uop~}e})=\mathrm{var}({e})\quad\mathrm{var}({e_{1}\mathtt{~bop~}e_{2}})=\mathrm{var}({e_{1}})\cup\mathrm{var}({e_{2}})\\[10.00002pt]
\mathrm{var}({[e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}]})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{var}({e_{i}})\qquad\mathrm{var}({e_{1}..e_{2}})=\mathrm{var}({e_{1}})\cup\mathrm{var}({e_{2}})\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\quad}l}P,Q&\ ::=&\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hskip-14.22636pt\textbf{processes}\\
&&\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{request}\\
&\;\;\ \big|&{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{accept}\\[1.84995pt]
&\;\;\ \big|&N\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{choice}\\[1.84995pt]
&\;\;\ \big|&\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\,{e}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{send}\\[1.84995pt]
&\;\;\ \big|&P;Q\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{sequential}\\[1.84995pt]
&\;\;\ \big|&~{e}~~P~~{Q}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{conditional}\\[1.84995pt]
&\;\;\ \big|&~{x}~~{\ell}~~{P}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{for}\\[1.84995pt]
&\;\;\ \big|&~N~~N\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{repeat}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\quad}l}N&\ ::=&\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hskip-14.22636pt\textbf{input-guarded processes}\\
&&\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}(x_{i}).P_{i}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{choices}\\[20.0pt]
S&\ ::=&\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hskip-14.22636pt\textbf{systems}\\
&&P\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt\\
&\;\;\ \big|&S{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}S\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{parallel}\\
&\;\;\ \big|&{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}[}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}]}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{queue}\\
&\;\;\ \big|&(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})S\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{restriction}\\
\end{array}

Figure 2.
Syntax of processes and systems.

The syntax of processes
P
, input-guarded non-deterministic
sequential processes
N
, and systems
S
is given in

Fig.

2
.
A process
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P
requests a new session on
a shared name
{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}
and then behaves as
P
; dually, process

{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P
accepts the request of a new session
from another process and then behaves as
P
.
A message
e
is sent on a session channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
by the process

\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\,{e}
.
Sequential composition and conditional are standard.
An input-guarded non-deterministic sequential processes
\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}(x_{i}).P_{i}
(conventionally denoted as when
I=\emptyset
) can branch over
P_{i}
when a message is received on the
session channel
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}
; we assume
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{j}
when
i\neq j\in I
.
Our language for processes provide two different constructs for
iterations

~{x}~~{\ell}~~{P}\qquad\text{and}\qquad~N~~\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}(x_{i}).P_{i}

Intuitively, the former realises the controllers of iterative global
types, while the latter is used for the remaining roles (
cf.

Section

7
).
A for-loop iterates the body
P
on the list
\ell
.
The body
N
in a repeat-until-loop is a process of the form

\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}(x_{i}).P_{i}
and it is repeated until a
message on one of the channels
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}
on the until guard is
received.

We set the following precedence rules:
~{\_}~~\_~~{\_}
,

~{\_}~~{\_}~~{\_}
and
~\_~~
have the lowest precedence while

\_.\_
has precedence over
\_;\_
so that, e.g., the term

~{e}~~P~~{{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{i}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).Q}
reads
~{e}~~P~~{({{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{i}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).Q)}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{i}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P;Q
reads
({{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{i}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P);Q
.

Systems consist of a parallel composition of process together with the
queues
{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}[}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}]}
that store the values
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}
sent over the
session channels
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
. Given
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{h}
pairwise
distinct session channels, we write

{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{h})}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}[}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}_{h}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}]}
to denote

{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}[}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}]}\mid\ldots\mid{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{h}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}[}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}_{h}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}]}
.
Names
\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}
are bound in
(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})S

and related to the shared name
{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}
.

The definition of the set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(\_)}
of
free names
is standard
but for shared names
{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}
which are also decorated to keep track
of roles; formally we define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(\_)}
on systems as

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}[}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}]})}=\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\}\qquad\qquad\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}((\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})S)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(S)}\setminus\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\qquad\qquad\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(S{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}S^{\prime})}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(S)}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(S^{\prime})}\end{array}

while for processes we have

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P)}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u},{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}^{{\mathsf{{0}}}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(P)}\setminus\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}({{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P)}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u},{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(P)}\setminus\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\,{e})}=\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\}\cup\mathrm{var}({e})&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}(x_{i}).P_{i})}=\bigcup_{i\in I}(\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(P_{i})}\setminus\{x_{i}\})\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(P;Q)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(P)}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(Q)}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(~{e}~~P~~{Q})}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(P)}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(Q)}\cup\mathrm{var}({e})\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(~{x}~~{\ell}~~{P})}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(P)}\setminus\{x\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(\ell)}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(~N~~N^{\prime})}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(N)}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(N^{\prime})}\end{array}

where, in the first equation, a process requesting a new session on

{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}
plays the role
0
and, in the second equation, a process
accepting on
{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}
plays role
{\mathsf{{p}}}
.

The set
\mathtt{fu}(S)
of
free shared names
of
S
is defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(S)}\cap\mbox{$\mathbb{U}$}
. Similarly the set of
free session names

\mathtt{fy}(S)
(resp.
free variables

\mathtt{fx}(S)
) of
S
is defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(S)}\cap\mbox{$\mathbb{Y}$}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{fn}(S)}\cap\mbox{$\mathbb{X}$}
). The set
\mathtt{bn}(\_)

of
bound names
is defined as

\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad\qquad}l}\mathtt{bn}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P)=\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\cup\mathtt{bn}(P)&\mathtt{bn}({{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P)=\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\cup\mathtt{bn}(P)\\
\mathtt{bn}(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\,{e})=\emptyset&\mathtt{bn}(\displaystyle{\sum_{i\in I}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}_{i}}(x_{i}).P_{i})=\bigcup_{i\in I}(\{x_{i}\}\cup\mathtt{bn}(P_{i}))\\
\mathtt{bn}(P;Q)=\mathtt{bn}(P)\cup\mathtt{bn}(Q)&\mathtt{bn}(~{e}~~P~~{Q})=\mathtt{bn}(P)\cup\mathtt{bn}(Q)\\
\mathtt{bn}(~{x}~~{\ell}~~{P})=\{x\}\cup\mathtt{bn}(P)&\mathtt{bn}(~N~~N^{\prime})=\mathtt{bn}(N)\cup\mathtt{bn}(N^{\prime})\\[10.0pt]
\mathtt{bn}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}[}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.2,0.3}\vec{\mathsf{v}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}]})=\emptyset&\mathtt{bn}((\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})S)=\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\cup\mathtt{bn}(S)\\
\mathtt{bn}(S{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}S^{\prime})=\mathtt{bn}(S)\cup\mathtt{bn}(S^{\prime})\end{array}

The set
\mathtt{by}(S)
of
bound session names
of
S
is defined as

\mathtt{bn}(S)\cap\mbox{$\mathbb{Y}$}
and the set
\mathtt{bx}(S)
of
bound variables
of

S
is
\mathtt{bn}(S)\cap\mbox{$\mathbb{X}$}
. Note that
\mathtt{bn}(S)\cap\mbox{$\mathbb{U}$}=\emptyset

for all
S
.

Figure 3.
Summary of notation for processes

Fig.

3
summarises the notation introduced so
far for the syntax of processes.

As customary, we rely on a
structural congruence relation

\equiv
defined as the least congruence over systems closed with
respect to
\alpha
-conversion and the following axioms

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1cm}}l}(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}{{}^{\prime}})S\equiv(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}{{}^{\prime}})(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})S\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})\equiv\\
(\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})(S{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}S^{\prime})\equiv S\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}\ (\nu\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}@}{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u})S^{\prime},\text{ when }\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\not\subseteq\mathtt{fy}(S)\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt\end{array}

and such that
\_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}\_
and
\_;\_
form monoids with identity
and the former is commutative.

The operational semantics of systems is given by the LTS inductively
defined by the rules in
Figs.

4
and
5
where

•

a store
\sigma
records both the values assigned to variables
and the session channels created by a process,

•

\sigma[{x}\mapsto{\mathsf{v}}]
is the update of

\sigma
at
x
with
\mathsf{v}
(and likewise for

\sigma[{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}]
), and

•

{e}\downarrow\sigma
is the evaluation of
e
(defined if

\mathrm{var}({e})\subseteq\mathrm{dom}{(\sigma)}
and undefined otherwise).
We assume that an expression
e
depends only on its variables, that
is, for all stores
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\sigma|_{\mbox{$\mathbb{X}$}}=\sigma^{\prime}|_{\mbox{$\mathbb{X}$}}\implies{e}\downarrow\sigma={e}\downarrow\sigma^{\prime}

where
\cdot|\_
is the standard restriction of a function on a
subset of its domain.

Labels are given by the following productions

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\ ::=\ \overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\mathsf{v}\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\mathsf{v}\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad\tau\quad\;\;\ \big|\ \;\;\quad e\vdash\alpha

(4.1)

that respectively represent the request of initialisation of a session
on
{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}
, the acceptance of joining a session on
{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}
with
role
{\mathsf{{p}}}
, the sending of a value on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
, the reception of a
value on
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}
, the silent step
\tau
, and
conditional

actions
e\vdash\alpha
where
e
is a boolean expression.
A conditional action labelling a transition
\langle{S},{}\rangle\xrightarrow{e\vdash\alpha}\langle{S^{\prime}},{\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle
denotes that
\langle{S},{}\rangle
performs
the action
\alpha
and moves to
\langle{S^{\prime}},{\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle
because
{e}\downarrow\sigma
holds.
We may write
\alpha
instead of
\mathtt{true}\vdash\alpha
and

e\land e^{\prime}\vdash\alpha
instead of
e\vdash(e^{\prime}\vdash\alpha)
.

Functions
\mathtt{fy}(\_)
and
\mathtt{bn}(\_)
extend to labels as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad\ }l@{\quad\ }l}\mathtt{fy}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}))=\mathtt{fy}({{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}))=\{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}\}&\mathtt{fy}(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\mathsf{v})=\mathtt{fy}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\mathsf{v})=\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\}\quad\mathtt{fy}(\tau)=\emptyset&\mathtt{fy}(e\vdash\alpha)=\mathtt{fy}(\alpha)\\[10.00002pt]
\mathtt{bn}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}))=\mathtt{bn}({{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{p}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}))=\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}&\mathtt{bn}(\overline{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\mathsf{v})=\mathtt{bn}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}\mathsf{v})=\mathtt{bn}(\tau)=\emptyset&\mathtt{bn}(e\vdash\alpha)=\mathtt{bn}(\alpha)\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1em}}r}\lx@intercol\hfil\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{[SReq]}$}\\
\frac{\textstyle\rule[-5.03749pt]{0.0pt}{11.62495pt}\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}\not\in\mathrm{dom}{(\sigma)}}{\textstyle\rule[-1.93748pt]{0.0pt}{11.62495pt}\langle{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}).P},{}\rangle\xrightarrow{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}^{{\mathsf{{n}}}}(\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}})}\langle{P},{\sigma[{\vec{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.1719,0.75}y}}}}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0.7813,0.3828,0.2031}u}}]}\rangle}\end{array}\hfill\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{[SAcc]}$}\\
